Initially he tried to cope by immersing himself in work, jogging, and solo trips to galleries, but over the next several months he began missing social outings with his board‑game group and stopped sharing his photography and sketches online, feeling increasingly disconnected from the activities that once anchored him. Approximately two years and three months ago, his ability to concentrate at his customer service job began to erode, he started calling in sick more often, and he let his meticulous budgeting and journaling habits lapse, leading to mounting financial stress and warnings from his supervisor about his declining performance. When a supervisor suggested a leave of absence after he broke down during a performance review and he realized he was no longer managing basic routines like cooking, paying bills on time, or maintaining his weekend hikes, Robert accepted a referral to a psychiatric clinic through his company’s health plan.\n- Chief Complaint: “I feel like I’ve lost my drive and personality—I don’t have the energy or motivation to do anything, even the art and routines that used to keep me going.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S019 (Delusions): For a long time now I’ve been convinced that my ex-wife and my old supervisor somehow got people at my new building and at work to watch me and report what I do, and even when I try to tell myself that sounds crazy, every sideways glance or whisper just feels like proof they’re still out to ruin me.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): Pretty much every day I hear this male voice, kind of behind my right ear, telling me I’m pathetic or that everyone can see what a failure I am, and even when I put on headphones or check the room it doesn’t stop or go away.\n- S022 (Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior): There are days when I’ll just suddenly freeze in the middle of the living room or at my desk at work, staring at one spot and not moving or answering people, like my body and brain have both jammed and I can’t get myself to respond until it passes.\n- S023 (Negative_Symptoms): Most of the time now I feel like I’m on mute inside and out—my face doesn’t match what I’m feeling, I barely say more than a few words to coworkers or friends, and even simple things like showering, cooking, or answering a text feel like they take more effort than I have.\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to be the guy organizing board‑game nights and posting my photos and sketches online, but over the last couple years I’ve stopped going to meetups, left group chats on read, and let my camera collect dust because I just don’t care enough to pick anything up anymore.\n- S026 (Weight_Appetite_Change): Since all this started I usually just eat one cheap, random meal late at night—instant noodles or whatever’s around—because I’m never really hungry, and my pants are hanging loose now from how much weight I’ve dropped without even trying.\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie awake for hours replaying conversations from the divorce or worrying that people are talking about me, then when I finally crash I wake up around 3 or 4 a.m. and just stare at the ceiling until my alarm goes off, feeling like I didn’t actually rest at all.\n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): Lately my body feels like it’s moving through glue—walking to the bus, picking up the phone at work, even reaching for my toothbrush is slowed down, and I’ll sit there with my hand halfway to something for what feels like forever before I actually finish the motion.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I constantly feel like I ruined my ex’s life, let everyone at my job down, and somehow even caused the tension between my parents, and I catch myself thinking I deserve to be watched and punished because I’m basically a burden who screws up everything I touch.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- psychotic_symptoms_and_depressive_episode_occur_concurrently: On most days for the last couple of years, it’s like everything piles on at once—I’m dragging myself through the day feeling empty and exhausted while that voice tears me down and I’m convinced people are watching and judging me wherever I go.\n- depressive_episode_majority_of_illness: Looking back since things really started falling apart after the divorce, the heavy, hopeless, drained feeling has basically been there the whole time, even during stretches when the voice or the paranoia eased up a bit.\n- delusions_and_hallucinations_occur_for_2_or_more_weeks_in_the_absence_of_a_major_mood_episode: There was a stretch last summer where I didn’t feel especially sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking most days and I was still sure my neighbors were reporting on me, even though my mood wasn’t as low as usual.\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this I’ve gone from being a solid employee and active with friends to barely keeping my job with repeated warnings, letting bills and basic chores pile up, and mostly isolating in my tiny apartment instead of seeing people.\n- duration: This has been building and then sticking around pretty much continuously since a few months after the divorce, so a little over two years now.\n- psychotic_symptoms_duration: The voice and the feeling that people are monitoring me have been a regular part of my life since the end of that first rough year after the divorce, so well over a year now.\n- depressive_episode_duration: The heavy depression—no energy, no motivation, feeling worthless—has been there most days since shortly after the divorce, for a bit over two years.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer that my ex-wife and my old supervisor somehow got people at my new building and at work to watch me and report what I do, and even when I try to tell myself that sounds crazy, every sideways glance or This persistence of psychosis during a period of milder/flat mood could point toward Schizoaffective disorder (F25) or Schizophrenia (F20) with negative/depressive symptoms, but we cannot yet rule out a Severe Depressive Episode with psychotic symptoms (F32.3) if the 'flatness' is interpreted as a continuation of the affective episode. The voice and the feeling that I was being watched didn't start until about a year after the split, when I was already deeply depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood and energy felt completely back to normal—not even flat or drained—but you were still hearing the voices or feeling paranoid?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never felt completely back to normal. The closest was last summer when I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking and I was still convinced my neighbors were monitoring me.\n\nDoctor: Could you describe what the voice sounds like and what kinds of things it typically says to you?\n\nPatient: It’s a male voice that always sounds like it's coming from right behind my right ear. It tells me that I'm pathetic and that everyone can see what a failure I am. Even when I put on headphones or check the room to see who's there, it doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were not your own, perhaps that they were being put into your mind, taken out, or broadcasted so that others could hear them?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. While my thoughts are often really dark and overwhelming, they still feel like they are my own. I've never felt like they were being put into my head, taken out, or broadcasted to others."

I've never felt like they were being put into my head, taken out, or broadcasted to others.\n\nDoctor: During those few weeks last summer when you felt 'flat' rather than deeply sad, did you still experience other depression symptoms like low energy, sleep issues, or feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, during that stretch last summer, I didn't really have those intense feelings of worthlessness or deep sadness—my mood was just flat and not as low as usual. But even though the heavy depression had eased up, the voice and the feeling that my neighbors were reporting on me were still very much there.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch last summer when your deep sadness eased up, were you able to experience any interest or enjoyment in your art or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, even though the intense sadness lifted a bit, I still didn't feel any real interest or joy in anything. I just felt completely flat and numb, so my camera and sketches just sat there collecting dust. I couldn't bring myself to care enough to pick them up or do any of the things I used to love."

The closest was last summer when I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking and I was still convinced my neighbors were monitoring me.\n\nDoctor: Could you describe what the voice sounds like and what kinds of things it typically says to you?\n\nPatient: It’s a male voice that always sounds like it's coming from right behind my right ear. It tells me that I'm pathetic and that everyone can see what a failure I am. Even when I put on headphones or check the room to see who's there, it doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were not your own, perhaps that they were being put into your mind, taken out, or broadcasted so that others could hear them?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. While my thoughts are often really dark and overwhelming, they still feel like they are my own. I've never felt like they were being put into my head, taken out, or broadcasted to others.\n\nDoctor: During those few weeks last summer when you felt 'flat' rather than deeply sad, did you still experience other depression symptoms like low energy, sleep issues, or feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, during that stretch last summer, I didn't really have those intense feelings of worthlessness or deep sadness—my mood was just flat and not as low as usual. But even though the heavy depression had eased up, the voice and the feeling that my neighbors were reporting on me were still very much there.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch last summer when your deep sadness eased up, were you able to experience any interest or enjoyment in your art or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, even though the intense sadness lifted a bit, I still didn't feel any real interest or joy in anything. I just felt completely flat and numb, so my camera and sketches just sat there collecting dust. I couldn't bring myself to care enough to pick them up or do any of the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have you or others noticed any changes in your ability to organize your thoughts, or has anyone mentioned that your speech has become difficult to follow or understand?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any issues with organizing my thoughts, and no one has ever told me that my speech is difficult to follow or understand. To be honest, I generally just don't speak very much to anyone these days anyway."

I've never had periods where I felt unusually energetic, hyper, or happy, or felt like I didn't need to sleep. It has really just been the opposite for me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and is this the first time you've experienced a period of depression like this?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for a little over two years now, starting not long after my divorce. Yes, this is definitely the first time I've ever experienced a depression like this, as my life and routines were pretty stable before everything fell apart.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have you had any periods lasting more than two months where your mood returned to normal and you felt like yourself again?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods where my mood went back to normal or I felt like myself again. That heavy, drained feeling has been there pretty much constantly for the entire two years since the divorce. Even during the times when the voice or the paranoia got a bit quieter, the depression never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a voice and feeling paranoid. Have you ever experienced these voices or paranoia during a time when you were not feeling depressed, or did they only start after the depression began?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Last summer there was a stretch of a few weeks where I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking to me most days. Even though my mood wasn't as low then, I was still completely convinced my neighbors were monitoring and reporting on me.\n\nDoctor: Before your depression and the divorce started two years ago, did you ever hear these voices or have these feelings that people were monitoring you?\n\nPatient: No, I never had those experiences before the divorce. The voice and the feeling that I was being watched didn't start until about a year after the split, when I was already deeply depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood and energy felt completely back to normal—not even flat or drained—but you were still hearing the voices or feeling paranoid?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never felt completely back to normal. The closest was last summer when I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking and I was still convinced my neighbors were monitoring me.\n\nDoctor: Could you describe what the voice sounds like and what kinds of things it typically says to you?\n\nPatient: It’s a male voice that always sounds like it's coming from right behind my right ear. It tells me that I'm pathetic and that everyone can see what a failure I am. Even when I put on headphones or check the room to see who's there, it doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were not your own, perhaps that they were being put into your mind, taken out, or broadcasted so that others could hear them?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. While my thoughts are often really dark and overwhelming, they still feel like they are my own. I've never felt like they were being put into my head, taken out, or broadcasted to others.\n\nDoctor: During those few weeks last summer when you felt 'flat' rather than deeply sad, did you still experience other depression symptoms like low energy, sleep issues, or feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, during that stretch last summer, I didn't really have those intense feelings of worthlessness or deep sadness—my mood was just flat and not as low as usual. But even though the heavy depression had eased up, the voice and the feeling that my neighbors were reporting on me were still very much there.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch last summer when your deep sadness eased up, were you able to experience any interest or enjoyment in your art or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, even though the intense sadness lifted a bit, I still didn't feel any real interest or joy in anything. I just felt completely flat and numb, so my camera and sketches just sat there collecting dust. I couldn't bring myself to care enough to pick them up or do any of the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have you or others noticed any changes in your ability to organize your thoughts, or has anyone mentioned that your speech has become difficult to follow or understand?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any issues with organizing my thoughts, and no one has ever told me that my speech is difficult to follow or understand. To be honest, I generally just don't speak very much to anyone these days anyway.\n\nDoctor: When the voice and the feeling of being watched first started a year ago, was your depression at its absolute worst, or did those experiences begin during a time when your mood felt a bit lighter?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure if my depression was at its absolute worst right when the voice and paranoia started, as that whole period is a bit of a blur. I just know I was already feeling very low, heavy, and exhausted by the end of that first year after the divorce."

I just know I was already feeling very low, heavy, and exhausted by the end of that first year after the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Why do you believe your neighbors are monitoring and reporting on you—do you feel it is somehow connected to you being a failure, or is there another reason?\n\nPatient: It’s actually both. I'm convinced that my ex-wife and my old supervisor somehow got people at my building and at work to watch me and report what I do to ruin me. But at the same time, I feel like I deserve this punishment because I really am a failure who let everyone down and ruined my ex's life."

Even during the times when the voice or the paranoia got a bit quieter, the depression never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a voice and feeling paranoid. Have you ever experienced these voices or paranoia during a time when you were not feeling depressed, or did they only start after the depression began?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Last summer there was a stretch of a few weeks where I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking to me most days. Even though my mood wasn't as low then, I was still completely convinced my neighbors were monitoring and reporting on me.\n\nDoctor: Before your depression and the divorce started two years ago, did you ever hear these voices or have these feelings that people were monitoring you?\n\nPatient: No, I never had those experiences before the divorce. The voice and the feeling that I was being watched didn't start until about a year after the split, when I was already deeply depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood and energy felt completely back to normal—not even flat or drained—but you were still hearing the voices or feeling paranoid?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never felt completely back to normal. The closest was last summer when I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking and I was still convinced my neighbors were monitoring me.\n\nDoctor: Could you describe what the voice sounds like and what kinds of things it typically says to you?\n\nPatient: It’s a male voice that always sounds like it's coming from right behind my right ear. It tells me that I'm pathetic and that everyone can see what a failure I am. Even when I put on headphones or check the room to see who's there, it doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were not your own, perhaps that they were being put into your mind, taken out, or broadcasted so that others could hear them?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. While my thoughts are often really dark and overwhelming, they still feel like they are my own. I've never felt like they were being put into my head, taken out, or broadcasted to others.\n\nDoctor: During those few weeks last summer when you felt 'flat' rather than deeply sad, did you still experience other depression symptoms like low energy, sleep issues, or feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, during that stretch last summer, I didn't really have those intense feelings of worthlessness or deep sadness—my mood was just flat and not as low as usual. But even though the heavy depression had eased up, the voice and the feeling that my neighbors were reporting on me were still very much there.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch last summer when your deep sadness eased up, were you able to experience any interest or enjoyment in your art or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, even though the intense sadness lifted a bit, I still didn't feel any real interest or joy in anything. I just felt completely flat and numb, so my camera and sketches just sat there collecting dust. I couldn't bring myself to care enough to pick them up or do any of the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have you or others noticed any changes in your ability to organize your thoughts, or has anyone mentioned that your speech has become difficult to follow or understand?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any issues with organizing my thoughts, and no one has ever told me that my speech is difficult to follow or understand. To be honest, I generally just don't speak very much to anyone these days anyway.\n\nDoctor: When the voice and the feeling of being watched first started a year ago, was your depression at its absolute worst, or did those experiences begin during a time when your mood felt a bit lighter?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure if my depression was at its absolute worst right when the voice and paranoia started, as that whole period is a bit of a blur. I just know I was already feeling very low, heavy, and exhausted by the end of that first year after the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Why do you believe your neighbors are monitoring and reporting on you—do you feel it is somehow connected to you being a failure, or is there another reason?\n\nPatient: It’s actually both. I'm convinced that my ex-wife and my old supervisor somehow got people at my building and at work to watch me and report what I do to ruin me. But at the same time, I feel like I deserve this punishment because I really am a failure who let everyone down and ruined my ex's life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications since these symptoms started, or do you have any medical conditions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't drink alcohol or use any recreational drugs, and I'm not taking any new medications. I also don't have any other known medical conditions."
  }

I couldn't bring myself to care enough to pick them up or do any of the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have you or others noticed any changes in your ability to organize your thoughts, or has anyone mentioned that your speech has become difficult to follow or understand?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any issues with organizing my thoughts, and no one has ever told me that my speech is difficult to follow or understand. I just know I was already feeling very low, heavy, and exhausted by the end of that first year after the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Why do you believe your neighbors are monitoring and reporting on you—do you feel it is somehow connected to you being a failure, or is there another reason?\n\nPatient: It’s actually both. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’ve lost my drive and personality—I don’t have the energy or motivation to do anything, even the art and routines that used to keep me going. It just feels like I'm completely empty most of the time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods lasting at least a few days where you felt the exact opposite—unusually energetic, happy, or active, perhaps needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like that. I've never had periods where I felt unusually energetic, hyper, or happy, or felt like I didn't need to sleep. It has really just been the opposite for me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and is this the first time you've experienced a period of depression like this?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for a little over two years now, starting not long after my divorce. Yes, this is definitely the first time I've ever experienced a depression like this, as my life and routines were pretty stable before everything fell apart.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have you had any periods lasting more than two months where your mood returned to normal and you felt like yourself again?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods where my mood went back to normal or I felt like myself again. That heavy, drained feeling has been there pretty much constantly for the entire two years since the divorce. Even during the times when the voice or the paranoia got a bit quieter, the depression never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing a voice and feeling paranoid. Have you ever experienced these voices or paranoia during a time when you were not feeling depressed, or did they only start after the depression began?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. Last summer there was a stretch of a few weeks where I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking to me most days. Even though my mood wasn't as low then, I was still completely convinced my neighbors were monitoring and reporting on me.\n\nDoctor: Before your depression and the divorce started two years ago, did you ever hear these voices or have these feelings that people were monitoring you?\n\nPatient: No, I never had those experiences before the divorce. The voice and the feeling that I was being watched didn't start until about a year after the split, when I was already deeply depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood and energy felt completely back to normal—not even flat or drained—but you were still hearing the voices or feeling paranoid?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never felt completely back to normal. The closest was last summer when I didn't feel deeply sad, just kind of flat, but the voice was still talking and I was still convinced my neighbors were monitoring me.\n\nDoctor: Could you describe what the voice sounds like and what kinds of things it typically says to you?\n\nPatient: It’s a male voice that always sounds like it's coming from right behind my right ear. It tells me that I'm pathetic and that everyone can see what a failure I am. Even when I put on headphones or check the room to see who's there, it doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts were not your own, perhaps that they were being put into your mind, taken out, or broadcasted so that others could hear them?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. While my thoughts are often really dark and overwhelming, they still feel like they are my own. I've never felt like they were being put into my head, taken out, or broadcasted to others.\n\nDoctor: During those few weeks last summer when you felt 'flat' rather than deeply sad, did you still experience other depression symptoms like low energy, sleep issues, or feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, during that stretch last summer, I didn't really have those intense feelings of worthlessness or deep sadness—my mood was just flat and not as low as usual. But even though the heavy depression had eased up, the voice and the feeling that my neighbors were reporting on me were still very much there.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch last summer when your deep sadness eased up, were you able to experience any interest or enjoyment in your art or other daily activities?\n\nPatient: No, even though the intense sadness lifted a bit, I still didn't feel any real interest or joy in anything. I just felt completely flat and numb, so my camera and sketches just sat there collecting dust. I couldn't bring myself to care enough to pick them up or do any of the things I used to love.\n\nDoctor: Have you or others noticed any changes in your ability to organize your thoughts, or has anyone mentioned that your speech has become difficult to follow or understand?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any issues with organizing my thoughts, and no one has ever told me that my speech is difficult to follow or understand. To be honest, I generally just don't speak very much to anyone these days anyway.\n\nDoctor: When the voice and the feeling of being watched first started a year ago, was your depression at its absolute worst, or did those experiences begin during a time when your mood felt a bit lighter?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure if my depression was at its absolute worst right when the voice and paranoia started, as that whole period is a bit of a blur. I just know I was already feeling very low, heavy, and exhausted by the end of that first year after the divorce.\n\nDoctor: Why do you believe your neighbors are monitoring and reporting on you—do you feel it is somehow connected to you being a failure, or is there another reason?\n\nPatient: It’s actually both. I'm convinced that my ex-wife and my old supervisor somehow got people at my building and at work to watch me and report what I do to ruin me. But at the same time, I feel like I deserve this punishment because I really am a failure who let everyone down and ruined my ex's life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications since these symptoms started, or do you have any medical conditions?\n\nPatient: No, I don't drink alcohol or use any recreational drugs, and I'm not taking any new medications. I also don't have any other known medical conditions.\n\nDoctor: How have your sleep and appetite been over these past two years? Have you noticed any significant changes, like sleeping too much, having trouble falling asleep, or losing weight?\n\nPatient: I've lost a lot of weight because I rarely feel hungry and usually just eat one cheap meal like instant noodles late at night, so my pants are hanging loose now. My sleep is also terrible; I lie awake for hours replaying the divorce or worrying, and then I wake up around 3 or 4 a.m. unable to fall back asleep, feeling completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt completely free of any depression, flatness, or low energy, yet you still heard the voice or felt paranoid?"

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
